- *Allen v. State Board of Elections*, 393 U.S. 544 — broad coverage of changes affecting electoral choice remains; elective-to-appointive selection is distinguished from these retained elected offices.
- *Perkins v. Matthews*, 400 U.S. 379 — changes to the electorate and voting arrangements remain covered; coverage remains distinct from discrimination merits.
- *Dougherty County Board of Education v. White*, 439 U.S. 32 — candidacy restrictions directly related to electoral choice remain covered.
- *McCain v. Lybrand*, 465 U.S. 236, and *City of Lockhart v. United States*, 460 U.S. 125 — changes in electoral arrangements remain within §5; neither decides ordinary internal governing duties.
- *NAACP v. Hampton County Election Commission*, 470 U.S. 166 — practical changes to election timing or procedure require proper review; postelection timing alone supplies no exemption here.

##### Whether the Attorney General's interpretation permits coverage without the statutory connection to voting

**Holding:** The Attorney General's interpretation of §5 is entitled to substantial consideration but cannot extend preclearance to governing changes that lack the statutory connection to voting. The proposed significant-transfer/different-constituencies test cannot substitute for that connection in these appeals.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's opinion of the Court, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Souter and Thomas; five Justices support this proposition in both appeals.

**Material application:** The rejected test concerns significant transfers of authority between officials responsible to substantially different constituencies. That formulation can reach governing responsibilities without identifying any change in voting and therefore cannot substitute for the required connection.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The distinction between §5 coverage and review of discriminatory effect remains intact. Administrative expertise retains substantial weight within the statutory voting boundary.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *United States v. Board of Commissioners of Sheffield*, 435 U.S. 110 — administrative interpretation retains substantial weight within the statutory voting boundary.
- *Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*, 467 U.S. 837 — preserved; deference does not authorize exceeding the statute's scope.
- *Beer v. United States*, 425 U.S. 130 — coverage and discriminatory-effect review remain separate inquiries.
- *South Carolina v. Katzenbach*, 383 U.S. 301 — §5's remedial function is preserved; it does not by itself establish coverage of every governing change.

### Prevailing parties and reasonable fees

#### Farrar v. Hobby, 506 U.S. 103 (1992)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-990.

**Decided:** December 14, 1992.

##### Enforceable nominal relief establishes eligibility

**Holding:** A civil-rights plaintiff who obtains an enforceable nominal-damages judgment against the defendant from whom fees are sought is a prevailing party under §1988. The judgment must directly benefit the plaintiff by changing the defendant’s legal obligation; its nominal amount does not defeat eligibility.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of White’s opinion, joined by the other eight Justices; all nine Justices support this controlling proposition.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Carey v. Piphus, 435 U.S. 247 — applied; nominal relief vindicates the established procedural violation without presumed compensatory injury.
- Hewitt v. Helms, 482 U.S. 755 — distinguished; favorable language without enforceable relief differs from a payment judgment.
- Rhodes v. Stewart, 488 U.S. 1 — distinguished; ineffective declaratory relief differs from a judgment directly benefiting the estate.
- Texas State Teachers Assn. v. Garland Independent School District, 489 U.S. 782 — applied and clarified; magnitude affects amount, not eligibility where nominal relief is enforceable.

##### Reasonable amount requires reconsideration on this review

**Holding:** When the court of appeals denies fees solely because a nominal-damages plaintiff is ineligible, correction of that error does not itself determine a reasonable amount. On this record, the fee judgment is vacated and remanded for preserved reasonableness objections and an explained Hensley assessment of actual success, allowing reduction to zero without automatically reinstating the prior award or requiring proportionality to damages.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of White’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter; five Justices control the remedy and stated fee principles.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Hensley v. Eckerhart, 461 U.S. 424 — applied; actual success, unrelated unsuccessful work and a reasoned discretionary assessment govern amount.
- City of Riverside v. Rivera, 477 U.S. 561 — applied within its authority; no mechanical proportionality requirement, without guaranteed fees for nominal relief.
- Blanchard v. Bergeron, 489 U.S. 87 — applied; the court must determine a reasonable statutory fee in the circumstances.
- City of Burlington v. Dague, June 24, 1992 — distinguished; contingency enhancement under the two environmental statutes does not decide this eligibility or remand question.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Eligibility does not guarantee a positive fee. Actual success, exclusion of unrelated unsuccessful work and an explained Hensley assessment govern; no damages-to-fees ratio or invented public benefit is permitted. The decision does not categorically forbid affirmance on a sufficient alternative fee ground in another case.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Vacate the fee judgment and remand for preserved reasonableness objections and lower-court review as necessary. The prior $280,000 award is not automatically restored; the result may be a reduced award or zero.

### Private conspiracies and clinic obstruction

#### Bray v. Alexandria Women’s Health Clinic, 506 U.S. 263 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 90-985.

**Decided:** January 13, 1993.

##### Private conspiracy requires purposeful interference with a privately protected right

**Holding:** A purely private conspiracy under the deprivation clause of §1985(3) must aim at interfering with a right protected against private as well as official encroachment; incidental effects are insufficient. Clinic obstruction applied to interstate and local patients alike does not establish that interference with interstate travel was a conscious objective merely because substantial numbers of patients crossed state lines.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Kennedy and Thomas: five direct supporters. Souter separately agrees with rejection of this theory without a formal join.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Griffin v. Breckenridge, 403 U.S. 88 — applied; coverage of private conspiracies retains purposeful, invidiously discriminatory deprivation requirements.
- United Brotherhood of Carpenters & Joiners v. Scott, 463 U.S. 825 — applied; a private conspiracy must target a privately protected right.
- United States v. Guest, 383 U.S. 745 — applied by analogy; injury to a traveler differs from purposeful interference with interstate travel.

##### Constitutional abortion liberty does not itself supply the private-right element

**Holding:** Constitutional protection of abortion choice against governmental interference does not itself create a right against the purely private obstruction proved here. Without necessary state involvement or another right protected against private encroachment, the deprivation clause cannot sustain this theory merely because the conspiracy deliberately seeks to prevent abortions.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Kennedy and Thomas: five direct supporters of this distinct sufficient ground addressing the alternative privacy theory. Souter separately accepts Scott’s private-right limitation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Scott, 463 U.S. 825 — applied; the underlying constitutional guarantee’s state/private distinction remains.
- Roe v. Wade, 410 U.S. 113 — preserved as carried forward and modified by Casey; no direct private-obstruction cause follows.
- Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey, June 29, 1992 — preserved; its six-step state-regulation framework does not change §1985(3)’s elements.
- Great American Federal Savings & Loan Assn. v. Novotny, 442 U.S. 366 — applied; §1985(3) does not itself create the substantive right.

##### Federal fees fall while independent state relief requires examination

**Holding:** The existing §1988 fee-and-cost award cannot stand on the rejected §1985(3) liability and is vacated. The injunction returns for examination of its state trespass/public-nuisance foundations, jurisdiction and lawful scope; federal merits failure alone neither sustains it nor requires its dissolution.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Kennedy and Thomas: five direct supporters. Souter agrees to remove the present federal basis but would additionally direct consideration of hindrance.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Farrar v. Hobby, December 14, 1992 — distinguished; enforceable federal nominal relief differs from reversal of the only adjudicated federal liability basis.
- Gibbs, 383 U.S. 715 — applied; pendent adjudication and support for particular relief remain distinct.

##### The unadjudicated hindrance theory is not resolved here

**Holding:** The Court does not adjudicate liability under §1985(3)’s separate clause concerning conspiracies to prevent or hinder constituted state authorities from giving or securing equal protection. That theory was not adjudicated below or fairly included in the granted deprivation-clause questions, and no finding establishes the required purpose to hinder state protection; the remand does not direct liability under that theory.

**Proposition-level authority:** Scalia Part V, joined by Stone-Zsela, White, Kennedy and Thomas: five direct supporters control scope and mandate. Ultimate substantive requirements of the separate clause remain undecided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Yee v. City of Escondido, April 1, 1992 — applied; a distinct claim outside the grant differs from additional argument on a presented claim.
- Kamen v. Kemper Financial Services, Inc., 500 U.S. 90 — applied at that scope; additional legal arguments do not erase claim limits.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Women’s status as a qualifying class, invidious sex-based animus, the full nonracial class boundary, a categorical rule for equal intrastate obstruction and statutory authority for an injunction on a successful federal claim remain unresolved. Scalia Part III has four votes and supplies no alternative holding. The hindrance theory receives no merits ruling; a later properly pleaded and supported claim is not barred on its merits by this disposition.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse deprivation-clause liability, vacate the $27,687.55 federal fee-and-cost award and remand for the injunction’s state trespass/public-nuisance grounds, pendent jurisdiction and lawful scope. Federal merits failure does not automatically dissolve the injunction or dismiss the entire action for lack of jurisdiction. The five-Justice mandate directs no hindrance adjudication.

For Bray v. Alexandria Women’s Health Clinic, January 13, 1993, No. 90-985, concerning federal merits failure and pendent jurisdiction, see **Federal Courts — Federal merits failure and pendent jurisdiction**.

### Rational basis for economic classifications

#### FCC v. Beach Communications, Inc., 508 U.S. 307 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-603.
**Decided:** June 1, 1993.

For the other question-level holdings in FCC v. Beach Communications, Inc., June 1, 1993, No. 92-603, see Federal Courts — Legislative reenactment and mootness.

##### Common-owner bargaining and regulatory costs sustain the economic line

**Holding:** For an economic classification involving neither a suspect class nor a fundamental right under the question reviewed, a reasonably conceivable legitimate basis suffices; the challenger must negate such a basis, and formal legislative findings are unnecessary. Congress could rationally distinguish these commonly owned, controlled or managed dwellings from separately owned properties because bargaining arrangements and the relationship between regulatory cost and consumer protection differ; Moreno and Cleburne continue to forbid irrational or impermissibly motivated classifications.

**Proposition-level authority:** Thomas’s Parts I and II-A; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (8 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- United States Railroad Retirement Board v. Fritz, 449 U.S. 166: conceivable legitimate grounds and imperfect economic fit applied.
- Sullivan v. Stroop, 496 U.S. 478: ordinary review of social and economic classifications applied.
- Nordlinger v. Hahn, June 18, 1992: the rational-basis holding applied without converting its particular tax grounds into universal immunity from review.
- Department of Agriculture v. Moreno, 413 U.S. 528: illegitimate-purpose limit preserved.
- City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, 473 U.S. 432: irrational-prejudice limit preserved.

##### Competitive entry independently supports the ownership distinction

**Independent alternative holding:** Congress could independently distinguish separately owned buildings because an incumbent provider can add nearby buildings at a lower marginal cost than a new entrant can incur to install its own reception facilities, creating a plausible first-mover advantage. This conceivable competition concern is sufficient under ordinary rational-basis review without proof that every regulated system possesses monopoly power.

**Proposition-level authority for alternative holding:** Thomas’s Part II-B; White, Blackmun, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (7 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The regulatory-cost and competition grounds are independently sufficient. No First Amendment or heightened-scrutiny question is decided, and no actual monopoly is found.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the equal-protection invalidation; the separate questions not decided may receive further proceedings.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Fritz, 449 U.S. 166: a second conceivable legitimate economic basis independently sustains the line.
- Nordlinger, June 18, 1992: multiple sufficient rational grounds and tolerance for imperfect fit applied within ordinary review.

#### General Motors Corp. v. Tracy

**Docket or dockets:** No. 95-1232.
**Decided:** February 18, 1997.

For the separate questions in General Motors Corp. v. Tracy, No. 95-1232 decided February 18, 1997, see Federal Courts — Standing and personal injury (The assessed purchaser may bring this challenge); Constitutional Structure — Discriminatory state taxation (This record does not establish the asserted discrimination).

##### The economic classification has a rational basis

**Holding:** Ohio may distinguish regulated local distribution companies from independent natural-gas marketers under ordinary economic equal-protection review because the companies' actual rate and service duties rationally relate to the tax classification. Competitive overlap does not eliminate that legitimate distinction, and this rational-basis holding does not reduce the independent Commerce Clause requirements.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Souter's opinion, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer; seven merits votes. Stone-Zsela and Stevens do not reach this question.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No exact tax offset is established. Ordinary economic rationality does not satisfy or diminish independent Commerce Clause requirements.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** FCC v. Beach Communications supplies the reasonably conceivable legitimate-basis inquiry without demanding perfect fit or legislative findings; its application does not replace scrutiny under other constitutional provisions.

### Functional limits on absolute immunity

#### Antoine v. Byers & Anderson, Inc., 508 U.S. 429 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-7604.
**Decided:** June 7, 1993.

##### The ministerial transcript function does not carry absolute judicial immunity

**Holding:** Court reporters performing the statutory task of accurately recording or producing a verbatim transcript do not receive absolute judicial immunity merely because their work is important or integral to a judicial proceeding. A claimant must establish the historically and functionally justified immunity; this ministerial task does not involve the discretionary adjudication that supports judicial immunity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at this level of generality; no Marks synthesis.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The burden rests on the person claiming immunity. An authorized federal damages action, actionable deprivation, causation, qualified immunity or other defenses and damages remain undecided.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse the absolute-immunity dismissal and remand for the remaining questions through an authorized cause of action; no automatic liability.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Forrester v. White, 484 U.S. 219: functional distinction between adjudication and administration applied to reporting.
- Butz v. Economou, 438 U.S. 478: burden of justifying absolute immunity and functional comparison applied.
- Burns v. Reed, 500 U.S. 478: historical and functional justification required; importance alone is insufficient.
- Imbler v. Pachtman, 424 U.S. 409: immunity for adjudicative functions distinguished from the ministerial transcript task.
- Wyatt v. Cole, May 18, 1992: private-party qualified-immunity holding preserved without extension to the defense decided here.

#### Bogan v. Scott-Harris

**Docket or dockets:** No. 96-1569.

**Decided:** 1998-03-03.

##### Local legislative work receives absolute personal damages immunity

**Holding:** Local officials performing genuine legislative functions have absolute immunity from personal damages under §1983 for those acts. The protection follows the function, so an executive official participating in that legislative process receives it for legislative work without immunity for all executive conduct.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Protection is personal damages immunity for the legislative function, not municipal immunity, immunity for all executive conduct, or a ruling on every prospective remedy.

##### This budget ordinance is legislative in substance and form

**Holding:** Roderick’s committee work and vote, and Bogan’s introduction of the budget and signature on the abolition ordinance, are legislative acts here because they enact prospective policy concerning the city’s departments, offices and resources. The retaliatory-motive verdict does not transform those otherwise legislative functions into administrative acts or defeat absolute immunity.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Thomas), unanimous.

**Material application:** The ordinance prospectively eliminated the department and office within a broader budget and resource allocation, even though only one employee held that office. The Court accepts rather than reweighs the retaliation verdict.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Formal enactment alone is not decided sufficient. Separate administrative implementation, personal discharge decisions, ministerial enforcement and unclassified threats receive no automatic protection; immunity is not a declaration of constitutionality.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The two reviewed personal damages judgments are reversed on absolute immunity. The city verdict is not restored and no new evidentiary hearing is required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Tenney supplies protection of the legislative process and the rule that improper motive does not defeat immunity for legislative acts.
- Lake Country Estates supports function-based legislative immunity below the state-legislature level; the principle extends here to local lawmaking.
- Consumers Union supports equivalent protection for executive participation in the legislative function.
- Antoine preserves the obligation of the official claiming immunity to establish historical and functional justification; importance or office alone remains insufficient.
- Forrester’s administrative personnel action is distinguished from prospective abolition of a department and office.
- Fletcher preserves separate classification of actual functions and the motive/function distinction. Richardson’s different private-prison setting does not negate historically supported public legislative immunity. Clinton’s function-based analysis is consistent, but its distinct presidential constitutional rationale is unnecessary here.

### Pension withdrawal liability and fair adjudication

#### Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, 508 U.S. 602 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-904.
**Decided:** June 14, 1993.

For the other question-level holdings in Concrete Pipe & Products of California, Inc. v. Construction Laborers Pension Trust for Southern California, June 14, 1993, No. 91-904, see Property and Economic Rights — Pension withdrawal liability and takings.

##### The preliminary assessment is not an adjudication

**Holding:** The interested plan sponsor’s withdrawal-liability assessment and demand are enforcement acts, not an adjudication. Due process permits that preliminary demand because the dispute first receives adjudication before a neutral arbitrator, under the lawful arbitral burdens stated separately below.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The preliminary assessment is not an adjudication portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Tumey, 273 U.S. 510 — interested adjudication forbidden; the sponsor is not the adjudicator here.
- Ward, 409 U.S. 57 — appellate review does not automatically cure initial biased adjudication; that premise is absent here.
- Withrow, 421 U.S. 35 — preliminary and adjudicative functions distinguished.

##### The factual presumption assigns ordinary persuasion

**Holding:** In neutral arbitration, §1401(a)(3)(A) places on the employer the burden to disprove the challenged factual determination by a preponderance. It does not add appellate deference to the sponsor’s factual determinations, a clear-and-convincing requirement or a conclusive presumption.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, The factual presumption assigns ordinary persuasion portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter (8 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Crowell v. Benson, 285 U.S. 22 — constitutionally sound construction used within the interpretive choice the text permits.
- Section 1401(c) — judicial review presumes arbitral findings correct unless rebutted by a clear preponderance; no transfer of that standard into arbitration.

##### Actuarial rebuttal retains both statutory routes

**Holding:** Under §1401(a)(3)(B), a contesting employer may rebut the actuarial calculation by a preponderance showing either that assumptions and methods were unreasonable in the aggregate, considering plan experience and reasonable expectations, or that the actuary made a significant error in applying them. The professional and statutory constraints make this presumption constitutionally permissible here; fraud need not be proved.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, Actuarial rebuttal retains both statutory routes portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Section 1393 — statutory constraints on actuarial assumptions and methods applied.
- Section 1401(a)(3)(B) — aggregate unreasonableness OR significant application error preserved as separate routes.
- Connolly, 475 U.S. 211 — pooled-plan structure provides context, not an irrebuttable actuarial presumption.

##### This withdrawal allocation is rational

**Holding:** Allocating a proportionate share of unfunded vested-benefit costs to this withdrawing employer is rational in light of its participation in the pooled pension undertaking. Neither private contribution limits nor the absence of a one-to-one match between its contributions and benefits to its own employees defeats this substantive-due-process application.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, This withdrawal allocation is rational portion; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct proposition-level majority; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Arbitral factfinding and actuarial judgments remain distinct. Under the applicable §1393(a)(1) route, assumptions and methods collectively must be reasonable and represent the actuary’s best estimate of anticipated plan experience; another reasonable estimate alone proves no defect. Judicial review under §1401(c) separately presumes arbitral facts correct unless rebutted by a clear preponderance. Defined-benefit classification is outside review.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm enforcement of this award under independent arbitration and the stated statutory construction; no second arbitration is required.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Usery v. Turner Elkhorn, 428 U.S. 1 — rational retrospective economic allocation applied.
- PBGC v. R. A. Gray, 467 U.S. 717 — pension-protection allocation sustained within actual statutory setting.
- Connolly, 475 U.S. 211 — pooling and contractual limits treated consistently with its constitutional holding.

### Equality and procedure in civil commitment

#### Heller v. Doe, 509 U.S. 312 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-351.
**Decided:** June 24, 1993.

##### The lower overall proof protection lacks a rational relation to the disadvantage imposed

**Holding:** Kentucky’s clear-and-convincing burden for involuntary commitment of adults with intellectual disability, compared with proof beyond a reasonable doubt for mental illness, violates equal protection on the preserved rational-basis challenge: the offered diagnostic, prediction and treatment differences do not rationally justify assigning this class the greater risk of mistaken confinement. Addington’s federal minimum remains unchanged; the remedy must address lawful equalization and severability rather than impose a national reasonable-doubt standard or order every class member released.

**Proposition-level authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter (5 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- City of Cleburne v. Cleburne Living Center, 473 U.S. 432: rational relationship to the actual disadvantage required; no new scrutiny tier adopted.
- Addington v. Texas, 441 U.S. 418: the clear-and-convincing minimum survives; unequal safeguards remain separately reviewable.
- FCC v. Beach Communications, June 1, 1993: no formal-findings or empirical-certainty requirement imposed; its specific economic justification does not decide this classification.
- Foucha, May 18, 1992: current lawful confinement predicates preserved; noncontrolling equal-protection reasoning is not promoted to law.

##### Family-party participation has a distinct rational basis

**Holding:** Kentucky may distinguish the two commitment proceedings by allowing guardians and immediate family members to participate as parties for adults with a lifelong developmental disability. Continuing family knowledge rationally supports that participation; the availability of testimony or a less extensive form of participation does not by itself defeat ordinary rational-basis review.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Cleburne, 473 U.S. 432: relevant differences may support a particular procedural distinction.
- Parham v. J.R., 442 U.S. 584: family information may be useful; adult confinement is not delegated to relatives.
- Schweiker v. Wilson, 450 U.S. 221: ordinary review does not require the least restrictive legislative alternative.

##### Party participation does not itself deprive the adult of fair adjudication

**Holding:** Allowing relatives or guardians to participate as parties does not categorically violate procedural due process where the adult has counsel and an effective opportunity to contest adverse evidence before an independent decisionmaker. Relatives may neither supply a substitute for the statutory commitment grounds nor control the decision or suppress the adult’s position.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No new scrutiny tier or national reasonable-doubt requirement is adopted; heightened scrutiny and Kentucky’s repealed voluntary-admission rule remain outside review. Party status is sustained categorically, not every hearing’s fairness.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm invalidation of unequal proof burdens and remand for severability and lawful equalization; reverse categorical invalidation of family-party participation subject to counsel, effective contest and independent adjudication. No blanket release.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Mathews v. Eldridge, 424 U.S. 319: private interest, error risk and safeguards, and government interests applied separately from equality review.
- Parham, 442 U.S. 584: informational participation distinguished from final decisional control.

### Prosecutorial investigation and press statements

#### Buckley v. Fitzsimmons, 509 U.S. 259 (1993)

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-7849.
**Decided:** June 24, 1993.

##### Investigative fabrication is not protected by absolute prosecutorial immunity

**Holding:** A prosecutor who performs ordinary investigative work to obtain evidence identifying a perpetrator before probable cause has no absolute prosecutorial immunity for alleged fabrication in that function; later courtroom use does not transform the earlier work into advocacy. Genuine evaluation and preparation in an advocate’s role remain protected, and neither all pre-indictment acts nor all post-indictment acts receive a categorical classification.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas (6 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Imbler v. Pachtman, 424 U.S. 409: protected advocacy and genuine preparation preserved; ordinary investigation distinguished.
- Burns v. Reed, 500 U.S. 478: function-specific limits applied to investigation.
- Forrester v. White, 484 U.S. 219, and Antoine, June 7, 1993: function and claimant’s burden control; importance to litigation is insufficient.

**Later-authority backlinks:** Fletcher v. Kalina, June 16, 1997, Ninth Circuit No. 95-36129: the functional distinction extends to a prosecutor’s personal arrest-warrant certification while preserving protected advocacy.

##### A press conference does not carry an advocate’s absolute immunity

**Holding:** A prosecutor’s statements to the press announcing an indictment have no absolute prosecutorial immunity merely because they concern a pending prosecution or cause injury through later judicial proceedings. The ordinary qualified-immunity inquiry and the requirement of an actionable constitutional deprivation remain separate.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s Opinion of the Court, this proposition; Stone-Zsela, White, Blackmun, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas (9 Justices). Direct majority support at the stated level of generality; no Marks aggregation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Allegations are not findings. Genuine advocacy preparation and unreviewed in-court presentation immunity remain; paid or coerced witness allegations require initial treatment below. Qualified immunity, constitutional injury, causation and damages are unresolved; defamation alone is not held actionable.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Reverse and remand: no absolute immunity for the pleaded preliminary fabrication (6–3) or press statements (9–0). No liability or witness-immunity change.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Imbler, 424 U.S. 409, and Burns, 500 U.S. 478: press activity lacks the protected advocacy function.
- Tower v. Glover, 467 U.S. 914: policy alone cannot create a new §1983 absolute immunity.
- Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800, and Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635: separate qualified-immunity law left unchanged.

#### Fletcher v. Kalina

**Docket or dockets:** Ninth Circuit No. 95-36129; no Supreme Court docket supplied.
**Decided:** June 16, 1997.

##### Protected prosecutorial advocacy remains distinct from personal evidentiary acts

**Holding:** A prosecutor has absolute immunity under §1983 for preparing and filing an information or warrant motion, evaluating evidence for those submissions, and presenting the State's legal case when performing an advocate's function. An allegation of bad purpose or false evidence does not remove that protection from the advocacy function, but the protection does not extend to a distinct personal evidentiary certification merely because the same prosecutor performs both acts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part I, joined in full by O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg and Breyer: eight Justices. Stone-Zsela joins protection of legitimate advocacy and separation of the personal evidentiary act, but not refusal of an additional dishonesty exception.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The function, rather than a categorical pre-charge or post-charge boundary, determines immunity. No general dishonesty exception to otherwise protected advocacy is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Imbler preserves charging and courtroom advocacy; Burns separates legal presentation from other acts. Buckley preserves genuine preparation while excluding ordinary investigation; Antoine requires historical and functional justification rather than importance to adjudication.

##### A prosecutor personally certifying arrest-warrant facts acts as a complaining witness

**Holding:** A prosecutor who personally swears to factual assertions offered to establish probable cause for an arrest warrant performs the evidentiary function of a complaining witness and has no absolute prosecutorial immunity for that certification. This classification depends on the personal evidentiary undertaking, not on proof of falsity, and does not withdraw immunity from ordinary courtroom witness testimony.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens's Part II, unanimous.

**Material application:** Kalina personally guaranteed the facts offered to obtain Fletcher's arrest warrant; that evidentiary undertaking is separate from preparing and presenting the charging papers.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Neither actual falsity nor the availability of another truthful affiant determines the functional classification. Ordinary courtroom testimony immunity remains intact.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm the denial of absolute immunity for the personal certification and remand, while preserving immunity for protected legal preparation, filing and presentation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Malley supplies the warrant-affiant comparison; official prosecutorial title does not immunize the same evidentiary function. Buckley and Antoine extend their functional distinction to the personal certification, without changing their other boundaries.

##### The function-specific immunity denial leaves constitutional liability and personal defenses unresolved

**Holding:** Denial of absolute immunity for the personal oath leaves Fletcher to establish an actionable constitutional deprivation, the required culpability, materiality, causation and compensable injury; Kalina retains qualified immunity and other available personal defenses. The existing qualified-immunity framework governs the remand, using the law and information relevant to the conduct at issue, without a new defense-burden rule or a finding that any element or defense has been established.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of Stevens's opinion, adopted in full by all eight Associate Justices. Stone-Zsela joins the unresolved-liability and bounded-remand propositions and accepts the existing qualified-immunity framework, without joining the source-specific causation and notice explanations.

**Limits and questions not reached:** A judge's warrant does not categorically break causation when material deception procured it, but a corrected truthful account may still establish probable cause. Neither question is resolved here. Disputed historical facts follow ordinary procedural rules; legal immunity is for the judge. No damages judgment or mandatory trial follows.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Hafer preserves personal-capacity liability only on proof of the officer’s own violation and preserves personal defenses. Harlow and Anderson retain objective, particularized clearly-established-law protection. Elder requires consideration of all relevant precedent; Lanier rejects categorical source and factual-twin restrictions, without supplying retrospective notice.

### Appellate sanctions and preserved review

#### McKnight v. General Motors Corp., 511 U.S. 659

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1113.

**Decided:** 1994-05-23.

For the other questions in McKnight v. General Motors Corp., 1994-05-23, No. 92-1113, see Federal Courts — Preserving a substantial question for further review is not itself frivolous.

##### Sanction relief leaves the underlying employment disposition undisturbed

**Holding:** Rivers governs the separate merits question: §101 of the Civil Rights Act of 1991 does not extend its new §1981 coverage to pre-enactment conduct, while claims already covered by the old statute retain their own scope. Vacating counsel's sanction neither reinstates McKnight's employment award nor creates a right to reopen the restored final judgment under Rule 60(b)(6); the underlying dismissal remains undisturbed.

**Proposition-level authority:** Per curiam Opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; nine direct joins.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., April 26, 1994: applies its nonretroactivity rule for new §101 coverage while preserving preexisting §1981 claims in their own scope.
- Landgraf v. USI Film Products, April 26, 1994: distinguishes the separate §102 damages question rather than treating §102 as the §1981 amendment.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, June 18, 1993: preserves completed judgments; it does not establish an automatic reopening entitlement.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Later resolution of the preserved merits question in Rivers does not retroactively establish frivolousness. The rule protects a substantial unresolved question properly preserved for higher review, not objectively baseless litigation or independently supported misconduct. No general construction of the circuit rule or Rule 60(b)(6) is adopted.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Only the $500 sanction against John S. Williamson, Jr., payable to the circuit clerk, is vacated and remanded for reconsideration. No independent abuse is found, no automatic sanction or reimbursement ordered, and the restored employment dismissal and separate Rule 60(b)(6) posture remain undisturbed.

### Civil statutory temporal reach

#### Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 511 U.S. 244

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-757.

**Decided:** 1994-04-26.

For the other questions in Landgraf v. USI Film Products, 1994-04-26, No. 92-757, see Federal Courts — Determine statutory temporal reach before applying the presumption against new retrospective consequences.

##### Section 102 does not add its new damages to this preenactment conduct

**Holding:** Section 102’s new compensatory and punitive liability does not attach to discriminatory conduct completed before November 21, 1991, because it adds legal consequences without sufficient congressional direction for that reach. Its Title VII compensation supplements, rather than replaces, §706(g) relief and excludes backpay, interest on backpay and other relief authorized by that provision; punitive damages have the distinct statutory predicates of malice or reckless indifference to federally protected rights and exclude government, government-agency and political-subdivision respondents. No such damages award or predicate finding is made here.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s §102 application, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, controls by five votes. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas independently reach the same unavailability of new damages through their concurrence. Blackmun disagrees about compensation and reserves punitive damages.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Bennett and Bowen: applied to the new monetary burden attached to completed conduct.
- Bradley: distinguished; the collateral fee award did not create this new compensation or punishment for the underlying private conduct.
- Franklin v. Gwinnett County Public Schools, 503 U.S. 60 (1992): distinguished; appropriate relief in an implied Title IX action does not enlarge Title VII’s earlier express remedial scheme.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, 503 U.S. 429 (1992): distinguished; its express substitution of law governing identified pending disputes does not supply the temporal direction missing here.

##### The jury election depends on an available statutory damages claim

**Holding:** Under §102(c), if the complaining party seeks compensatory or punitive damages available under that section, any party may demand a jury trial, and the court must not inform the jury of the statutory monetary limits. Because §102 damages are unavailable for this preenactment conduct, its conditional jury provision supplies no independent right to a new trial; this conclusion does not prohibit applying a genuinely procedural change to an appropriate future procedural act in another case.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s jury portion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, supplies the controlling five-Justice explanation. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reach the same conditional conclusion in their concurrence; Blackmun would permit a jury election because he would make compensation available.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Kaiser: applied to the enacted condition linking the jury option to §102 damages.
- Bradley: distinguished; current-law treatment of collateral matters does not erase an express condition on a new remedy or automatically reopen a completed trial.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The rule is provision-specific, not a declaration that the whole 1991 Act is inapplicable in pending cases. Section 402(a)'s enactment-date clause, §109(c)'s later-conduct limitation for extraterritorial amendments, and §402(b)'s exception for disparate-impact complaints filed before March 1, 1975 with initial decisions after October 30, 1983 remain distinct. Judicial retroactivity under Harper does not control legislative reach. An express congressional direction concerning the temporal operation of a statute resolves the threshold statutory question, subject to applicable constitutional limits. Such a direction does not alone determine whether Congress may constitutionally require reconsideration of an already completed Article III judgment. The present case concerns statutory consequences for earlier conduct in litigation properly before the courts and does not decide that separate final-judgment issue.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fifth Circuit judgment is affirmed, 8–1. No new damages trial, award or remand is ordered, and the existing equitable-remedy judgment and unreviewed constructive-discharge findings remain intact.

### Civil-rights amendments and earlier conduct

#### Rivers v. Roadway Express, Inc., 511 U.S. 298

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-938.

**Decided:** 1994-04-26.

##### Section 101’s new contractual coverage does not govern the earlier conduct

**Holding:** Section 101’s expansion of §1981’s making-and-enforcing-contracts coverage to include making, performance, modification, termination, and enjoyment of all benefits, privileges, terms and conditions of the contractual relationship does not govern conduct completed before November 21, 1991: it adds substantive legal consequences without sufficient congressional direction for that reach. This provision-specific rule does not eliminate a claim already covered by old §1981 or decide a separate dispute over subsection (c)’s protection against nongovernmental discrimination and impairment under color of state law.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s opinion, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; these five adopt the complete explanation. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas reach the same temporal result through their separate relevant-activity and textual-command method.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Landgraf v. USI Film Products, decided earlier April 26, 1994: applied to §101 independently of §102, preserving the provision-specific temporal inquiry and its five-Justice controlling explanation.
- Bowen v. Georgetown University Hospital, 488 U.S. 204 (1988), and Bennett v. New Jersey, 470 U.S. 632 (1985): applied to new substantive consequences for completed events; no constitutional bar against sufficiently directed civil retrospective legislation is adopted.
- Bradley v. School Board of Richmond, 416 U.S. 696 (1974): distinguished in its collateral litigation-fee setting, not overruled.
- Patterson v. McLean Credit Union, 491 U.S. 164 (1989): applied as the governing construction of the earlier text within its scope; an old-law enforcement claim is not extinguished by refusing the later expansion.

##### Corrective legislative purpose does not itself give an amendment retrospective reach

**Holding:** When Congress expands statutory liability in response to a judicial interpretation, its corrective purpose or use of a new definition does not alone make the amendment govern earlier conduct; sufficient temporal direction remains necessary under Landgraf, and Congress may supply that direction within constitutional limits. Judicial construction of the earlier statute remains distinct: Harper governs application of that construction to open cases, and a defendant need not prove actual reliance on a later-issued judicial decision before invoking the enacted amendment’s limited temporal reach.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stevens’s opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg, controls by five votes at this explanatory level. Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas accept the judicial/legislative distinction through their separate textual method, not through a presumed full join.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Patterson: retained as the old-text construction relevant to the temporal question; Congress’s prospective expansion supersedes its narrower coverage for conduct governed by the amendment, without overruling it as the construction of earlier law.
- Harper v. Virginia Department of Taxation, 509 U.S. 86 (1993): applied to distinguish judicial retroactivity from later legislation; the lower court’s selective Chevron Oil balancing is not adopted.
- Robertson v. Seattle Audubon Society, 503 U.S. 429 (1992): distinguished because its express substitution of law for identified pending disputes supplied direction absent here.
- Frisbie v. Whitney, 9 Wall. 187 (1870), and Freeborn v. Smith, 2 Wall. 160 (1865): distinguished as instances of directed curative legislation; neither establishes automatic retrospective reach for a corrective amendment.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Congress's prospective expansion supersedes Patterson's narrow coverage for conduct governed by the amendment while leaving it the construction of earlier text. The Court does not reconsider Patterson, endorse the lower court's whole-Act generalization or selective Chevron Oil balancing, or extinguish an old-scope enforcement claim. The distinct §1981(c) question remains open.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Sixth Circuit judgment is affirmed and the case remanded, 8–1, preserving the existing old-law contract-enforcement jury remand. That remand retains Lytle's protection against preclusion by later Title VII bench findings; it is not §102's new jury option. No discrimination finding, damages or dismissal of the preserved claim is ordered, and the unreviewed related matters are not reopened.

### Constitutional torts and fractured authority

#### Albright v. Oliver, 510 U.S. 266

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-833.

**Decided:** 1994-01-24.

##### Presented substantive-due-process claim

**Authority of the judgment:** Dismissal of Albright's presented substantive-due-process claim is affirmed, 7–2: Stone-Zsela, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg support the judgment; Blackmun and Stevens dissent. No controlling rationale exists under Marks. The four-Justice specific-source plurality, Kennedy and Thomas's distinct prosecution/due-process rationale and Souter's additional-injury rationale do not form a demonstrated logical subset.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The judgment binds the parties but establishes no general Fourth Amendment displacement rule, state-remedy bar or synthesized substantive-due-process test. It does not adopt the Seventh Circuit's incarceration, employment-loss or palpable-consequence requirement. The abandoned Fourth Amendment theory, accrual, immunity, municipal attribution and a general fabrication claim are undecided. Earlier authority retains its existing force; uses in the separate rationales supply no new majority construction.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Dismissal of the presented federal claim stands. The state malicious-prosecution count's dismissal without prejudice is undisturbed. No Fourth Amendment claim is reinstated, and no damages or merits remand is ordered.

### Disability education and parental reimbursement

#### Florence County School District Four v. Carter, 510 U.S. 7

**Docket or dockets:** No. 91-1523.

**Decided:** 1993-11-09.

##### Appropriate parental placement and equitable reimbursement

**Holding:** When the public offer violates IDEA and the parents' unilateral private placement provides an education otherwise appropriate for the child under the Act, §1415(e)(2) permits equitable reimbursement even if that placement lacks state approval or does not satisfy every requirement in §1401(a)(18)'s definition of a publicly provided free appropriate education. Parents act at financial risk and must establish both public inadequacy and private appropriateness; neither their choice nor the child's progress alone guarantees reimbursement, the court must consider relevant equitable circumstances and a reasonable amount, equitable considerations may support reduction or denial of relief, and full reimbursement is inappropriate if the private cost was unreasonable.

**Proposition-level authority:** O'Connor's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg; O'Connor and those eight Justices supply nine votes for this rule and affirmance. Direct unanimous authority; no fractured-opinion inference.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- *School Committee of Burlington v. Department of Education*, 471 U.S. 359 (1985): applies its statutory reimbursement authority, financial-risk rule, and equitable discretion; lack of state approval of the parental placement is not an additional categorical bar.
- *Board of Education v. Rowley*, 458 U.S. 176 (1982): leaves the standard governing appropriate public education intact; the public program's inadequacy and Trident's appropriateness are settled for this review.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Public inadequacy and Trident's appropriateness are settled for this review. The four public-education conditions in §1401(a)(18) are public expense, supervision and direction without charge; state standards; appropriate education at the relevant school level; and conformity with an individualized education program. Their wholesale transfer to the corrective parental remedy is rejected; the rule does not abolish public-placement standards or establish a constitutional funding right.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fourth Circuit reimbursement judgment is affirmed. Equitable authority over reasonable amounts remains; no compulsory approval of Trident or new merits remand is ordered.

### Equal protection in jury selection

#### J.E.B. v. Alabama ex rel. T.B., 511 U.S. 127

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1239.

**Decided:** 1994-04-19.

##### Purposeful sex discrimination in state peremptory strikes

**Holding:** The State may not strike a prospective juror because of sex or the assumption that sex itself predicts competence or impartiality. Under the equal-protection requirement that a sex classification substantially serve an important governmental objective, a generalized prediction that men will favor the alleged father does not justify this exclusion; genuine individual, sex-neutral reasons remain available.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter and Ginsburg; six Justices adopt the rule and explanation. Kennedy concurs in the judgment and independently supports the operative prohibition on individual-right grounds.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Craig v. Boren, 429 U.S. 190 — applied: sex classifications require an important objective and substantial relationship.
- Mississippi University for Women v. Hogan, 458 U.S. 718 — applied to unjustified sex stereotypes and equal protection of men.
- Batson v. Kentucky, 476 U.S. 79 — extended to purposeful sex-based state peremptories; its racial rule remains intact.
- Powers v. Ohio, 499 U.S. 400 — applied for the excluded juror’s individual interest and the litigant’s ability to challenge discriminatory selection.
- Edmonson v. Leesville Concrete Co., 500 U.S. 614, and Georgia v. McCollum — relied on for juror equality within selection; the private-party sex-strike question is not decided.
- Taylor v. Louisiana, 419 U.S. 522 — distinguished: no proportionally representative petit jury is required by this decision.

##### Staged proof and the remand

**Holding:** A party challenging a peremptory strike as purposeful sex discrimination must first make the required prima facie showing; the striking party must then give a genuine sex-neutral explanation, which need not justify a challenge for cause. The trial court decides intentional discrimination with the ultimate burden on the objector; if Alabama cannot sustain the challenged strikes under that inquiry, the verdict must be set aside and a new trial held before a lawfully selected jury.

**Proposition-level authority:** Blackmun’s six-Justice Opinion of the Court adopts the procedure and conditional remedy; Kennedy independently agrees with the operative inquiry and remand, producing seven votes for reversal and remand.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Batson v. Kentucky, 476 U.S. 79 — its staged purpose inquiry is extended without requiring a cause-level explanation or an explanation for every strike.
- Georgia v. McCollum — its distinction between production and the objector’s ultimate persuasion burden is preserved.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The case involves State strikes, not an adjudication of private-party sex strikes. No proportional petit jury, cause-level explanation for every strike or stricter general sex-classification rule is required. A genuinely neutral reason correlated with sex is not automatically forbidden, while a neutral label does not defeat a supported pretext finding.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Alabama Court of Civil Appeals is reversed and the case remanded, 7–2. Apply the staged selection inquiry and order a lawfully selected new trial if the strikes cannot be sustained. No paternity finding, jury quota or automatic determination of pretext is made.

### Parole consideration and prospective relief

#### Cavanaugh v. Roller

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1510.

**Decided:** 1993-11-30.

For the other questions in Cavanaugh v. Roller, 1993-11-30, No. 92-1510, see Federal Courts — A live claim for the outstanding hearing; Criminal Procedure — The binding loss of annual reconsideration increases punishment on this record.

##### Section 1983 permits this opportunity-only claim

**Holding:** A state prisoner may seek prospective declaratory and injunctive relief under §1983 against responsible officials to restore the legally required frequency of discretionary parole consideration when the judgment neither orders release nor establishes an entitlement to release or a shorter period of custody. Habeas is not the exclusive channel for this claim, and no general state-remedy exhaustion requirement attaches to the properly brought §1983 action.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of O'Connor's opinion, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, and Souter; six Justices adopt the same rule and affirm this remedial channel.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Habeas remains exclusive for release-determining relief within Preiser's scope and is not declared unavailable here. This judgment restores consideration, not an entitlement to release or credits that themselves shorten custody. Independent due-process and state constitutional claims and the unappealed damages rulings are not decided.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Preiser v. Rodriguez, 411 U.S. 475 (1973): distinguishes its exclusive-habeas holding for relief determining immediate or speedier release; that rule remains controlling within its scope.
- Wolff v. McDonnell, 418 U.S. 539 (1974), and Gerstein v. Pugh, 420 U.S. 103 (1975): apply their distinction between process relief and a release order to this mandatory consideration opportunity.
- Patsy v. Board of Regents, 457 U.S. 496 (1982): applies its rejection of a general exhaustion prerequisite after determining that §1983 is an available channel; it does not itself decide that antecedent question.
- Chatman-Bey v. Thornburgh, 864 F.2d 804 (D.C. Cir. 1988) (en banc): declines to extend its federal eligibility/habeas analysis to require exclusive habeas for this limited state-prisoner remedy.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Fourth Circuit's prospective-relief judgment is affirmed, 6–3. Its remand for a declaration restoring annual reconsideration remains in force; the district court may issue an injunction only if necessary to secure consideration. No release, damages, immunity reconsideration, contempt finding or specific hearing date is ordered. The unappealed official- and individual-capacity damages dismissals remain undisturbed.

**Later-authority backlinks:** South Carolina Act No. 184, effective January 1, 1994: offense-date classification and savings/transition terms; no later mootness, completed hearing, judgment vacatur or displacement of the federal holding is established.

#### Johnson v. Rodriguez

**Docket or dockets:** Fifth Circuit Nos. 95-50879 and 96-50382; no separate Supreme Court docket assigned.

**Decided:** 1998-02-17.

##### Initial parole and confidential-file process

**Holding:** Texas's parole scheme creates no entitlement to initial release because its substantive eligibility conditions and required procedures do not meaningfully restrict the Board's authority to deny release. Absent that entitlement, claimed unreliability in confidential protest material does not establish the asserted procedural due-process right to file correction, disclosure or an adversarial parole hearing.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part I-A, joined by all nine Justices.

**Material application:** Eligibility rules constrain when Texas may grant parole without requiring release on specified facts. Victim notice and participation duties, mandatory consideration of a victim-impact statement, file-inclusion rules and confidentiality do not create the asserted release entitlement; file inclusion is not mandatory reliance on every allegation.

**Limits and questions not reached:** This initial-release holding does not govern community liberty already conferred or permit a separate unconstitutional penalty for protected activity.

##### Protest-letter equal protection

**Holding:** The protest-letter system establishes no purposeful unconstitutional differential treatment or irrational parole criterion on this record. The distinct class claim is therefore dismissed with prejudice; inaccurate or vindictive statements do not alone justify a prohibition on receiving or considering every protest statement.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part I-B, joined by all nine Justices.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court adopts no rule requiring a comparison class to predate government action and resolves no claim of knowing official falsification, racial discrimination, protected-litigation retaliation, disregard of a binding invalidation order or every individual false statement.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Dismissal with prejudice of the distinct protest-letter class claim is affirmed.

##### The separate writ-writer equality claim

**Holding:** The remanded writ-writer equality claim requires relevant similarly situated treatment and purposeful discriminatory adverse effect under the actual right asserted. A penalty for independently protected litigation is not justified merely by describing all litigiousness as unreformed behavior; generalized litigation references or historical bias by another agency do not establish the claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Kennedy's Part III, joined by all seven other Associates: eight direct joins. Stone-Zsela agrees in preserving this component on remand and adds no ground.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Comparative proof need not always be statistical; the absence of statistics alone is not dispositive. No new strict-scrutiny rule or liability finding follows.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The distinct writ-writer equality claim remains for remand under its own comparison, purpose and injury requirements.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Sandin is applied to genuine substantive constraints; mandatory procedures alone do not require initial parole.
- Young and Morrissey are distinguished because community liberty already conferred is different from initial discretionary release.
- Crawford-El and Mt. Healthy supply ordinary motive proof and the officials' distinct preponderance same-decision defense; neither is altered.
- Umbehr and Valot preserve the distinction between absence of benefit entitlement and a protected-right penalty; their separate employment or contractor rules are not imported into this prison case.
- Monell's governmental-attribution principle is not converted into a universal condition for every state-officer injunction. No precedent is overruled.

For this case’s other questions, see First Amendment (Retaliation for protected litigation; Necessary assistance and independent petition activity); Federal Courts (Standing, Board attribution and class relief; Dependent fees).

### Qualified-immunity review

#### Elder v. Holloway, 510 U.S. 510

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-8579.

**Decided:** 1994-02-23.

##### Appellate review must consider all relevant law on the preserved immunity issue

**Holding:** Whether the asserted federal right was clearly established when the officers acted is a legal question reviewed independently on appeal using all relevant precedent, including decisions neither cited to nor discovered by the district court. That duty does not admit a new claim or factual record, make every considered precedent controlling, or permit later decisions to supply notice retroactively; here the notice inquiry remains fixed at April 27, 1987.

**Proposition-level authority:** Ginsburg's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter and Thomas. All nine adopt the legal-authority rule and the limited reversal and remand.

**Limits and questions not reached:** The Court neither grants nor denies ultimate immunity, decides the factual or legal sufficiency of exigency, awards damages, orders new factfinding or an automatic trial, nor imposes a mandatory merits-first sequence. United States v. Al-Azzawy, 784 F.2d 890, is relevant circuit law predating the arrest; its rule concerning a compelled exit and its ultimate exigency disposition must both be evaluated. Depositions about access to guns and five days of advance arrest planning bear in different directions and are not resolved here. Florida warrants and the absence of an Idaho warrant are distinct facts; the Court does not decide that either alone disposes of the home-arrest question. The lower courts must distinguish a coerced exit from a genuinely voluntary appearance under the governing home-arrest doctrine. Ordinary preservation of claims and evidence remains separate from consideration of legal authority on this preserved claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Harlow v. Fitzgerald, 457 U.S. 800: applies objective clearly-established-law protection; the defense is preserved, not replaced by a new reliance-only rule.
- Mitchell v. Forsyth, 472 U.S. 511: applies the legal character of the immunity inquiry.
- Pierce v. Underwood, 487 U.S. 552: applies independent appellate review of legal questions; this later decision does not itself supply officers with notice in April 1987.
- Davis v. Scherer, 468 U.S. 183: distinguishes a requirement that the relevant federal right be clearly established from a rule excluding uncited authority; unrelated duties do not defeat immunity.
- Hunter v. Bryant, December 16, 1991: preserves judicial determination under ordinary summary-judgment constraints using the supported nonmovant version of the complete contemporaneous record; its later decision date supplies no retroactive notice.
- Anderson v. Creighton, 483 U.S. 635: preserves the particularized objective inquiry used in current review; because it was decided after April 27, 1987, it is not itself pre-arrest notice authority.
- Payton v. New York, 445 U.S. 573: retains its warrant protection against a nonconsensual home arrest absent exigent circumstances for the lower court's application; this decision does not resolve that application.

**Operative remedy or transition:** The Ninth Circuit is reversed and the case remanded for independent immunity review using all relevant law and the proper summary-judgment record. Ultimate immunity, exigency, Fourth Amendment liability and damages remain undecided; no automatic trial or new factfinding is ordered.

#### Ayeni v. Mottola

**Docket or dockets:** Supreme Court No. 94-1202 (Mottola v. Ayeni); Second Circuit Nos. 94-6041(L), 94-6047.
**Decided:** May 27, 1997.

For the present constitutional rule in Ayeni v. Mottola, May 27, 1997, No. 94-1202, see Criminal Procedure — Warrant execution and private observers.

##### The legal immunity appeal uses the assumed conduct and its actual date

**Holding:** On this legal collateral-order appeal, the Court assesses whether the assumed conduct was apparently unlawful under law existing on March 5, 1992; a present violation alone does not defeat immunity, and a factually identical earlier case is not indispensable. The appeal does not permit findings about Mottola's actual role or resolution of disputed liability facts.

**Proposition-level authority:** Opinion of the Court (Stone-Zsela), Parts I–II, unanimous.

**Limits and questions not reached:** No general mandatory merits-first sequence, agency or media damages extension, or §1983 holding is adopted.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Anderson protects reasonable mistakes without demanding identical facts; Elder, Pinder and Lanier guide review but supply no retrospective 1992 notice. Davis prevents statutory breach alone from defeating immunity. Johnson and Behrens preserve the legal/factual appellate boundary; FDIC v. Meyer keeps an agency action distinct.

##### Pre-search law clearly forbade the pleaded nonassisting home intrusion

**Holding:** On the pleaded facts, pre-March 5, 1992 law made the officer's gratuitous admission of nonassisting outsiders to expose private home life for an unrelated purpose apparently unlawful. Mottola is therefore not entitled to dismissal on qualified immunity for this claim, but responsibility, causation and compensable injury remain to be proved.

**Proposition-level authority:** Stone-Zsela's Parts III–IV, joined by Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; five direct joins.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Affirm denial of the pleaded immunity defense, 5–4; responsibility, causation and compensable injury require proof. Other force, detention, destruction, pre-warrant-entry and separately treated photograph issues, substantive due process, CBS, unserved agents and the criminal footage proceeding remain outside the decision. No broadcast injury is presumed.

**Treatment of earlier authority:** Bivens, Payton and Andresen supply concrete home and purpose-bound restraints; Matias, Rivera, Clouston and Wright contribute the pre-search documentary, household and assistance limits. Section 3105 confirms the distinction without substituting for constitutional notice. Soldal, Wilson and Bills postdate the search and supply no notice; Harlow–Anderson is preserved.

### Conviction-related damages and accrual

#### Heck v. Humphrey, 512 U.S. 477

**Docket or dockets:** No. 93-6188.

**Decided:** 1994-06-24.

##### Necessary invalidity on the present in-custody record

**Holding:** A state prisoner in Heck's position, in custody with a lawful opportunity to challenge the conviction, has no presently cognizable §1983 damages claim when success necessarily establishes the invalidity of that outstanding conviction or sentence, unless the judgment has undergone qualifying invalidation. The rule decides that setting; it does not make present habeas availability a universal element or decide plaintiffs who never had a realistic custody-review opportunity or whose custody has ended.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part I of Justice O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg. All nine directly adopt this bounded rule; the distinct sufficient rationales are not added together to obtain its support.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Preiser v. Rodriguez, 411 U.S. 475 (1973): its release-channel distinction informs the new, bounded damages prerequisite; its holding is not misdescribed as already deciding damages-only claims.
- Wolff v. McDonnell, 418 U.S. 539 (1974): preserves civil relief compatible with the operative conviction and custody judgment.
- Patsy v. Board of Regents, 457 U.S. 496 (1982): remains the rule against general §1983 exhaustion; this decision addresses present cognizability instead.

##### Statutory coordination supports this limited prerequisite

**Holding:** For the in-custody, conviction-negating damages claim described in Part I, coordination of §1983 with the available habeas channel supports favorable invalidation as a prerequisite, with the common-law malicious-prosecution analogy implementing that coordination. This is a new, limited restriction on §1983, not conversion of the damages complaint into habeas, a general exhaustion rule, or adoption of every malicious-prosecution element.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part II of O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens and Souter; five Justices adopt this sufficient statutory explanation at this limited scope. Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas and Ginsburg do not join Part II.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Preiser v. Rodriguez: extends statutory coordination to the particular damages claim while preserving the distinct relief and conditions of habeas.
- Wolff v. McDonnell: preserves its permissible civil-remedy category; factual overlap with prosecution does not remove a claim from that category.
- Patsy v. Board of Regents: distinguishes general exhaustion from the newly adopted prerequisite; exhaustion alone cannot satisfy it.
- Carey v. Piphus, 435 U.S. 247 (1978): applies common-law remedial guidance as an aid to construing the federal remedy, not a wholesale importation of a state tort.

##### Qualifying invalidation and deferred accrual

**Holding:** The claim barred under Part I becomes cognizable only when the conviction or sentence has been reversed on direct review, expunged by executive order, declared invalid by a state tribunal authorized to do so, or called into question by a federal court's issuance of a writ of habeas corpus. That claim accrues only upon such invalidation; exhaustion, unsuccessful review, or loss of a review opportunity is not invalidation, and neither immediate accrual with equitable tolling nor a preservation stay is required for this unaccrued claim.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part III of O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all nine directly adopt the four invalidation routes and the accrual consequence for the barred claim.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Carey v. Piphus: applies its common-law-informed approach to the federal remedy without imposing additional constitutional-liability elements.
- Patsy v. Board of Regents: preserves the distinction between exhaustion and existence of a cognizable damages claim.

##### Independent injuries, claim-specific analysis and this dismissal

**Holding:** A claim whose success does not necessarily establish invalidity falls outside this prerequisite even if it concerns the same investigation or trial; courts must compare the civil claim's elements with the basis of the conviction, and compensatory recovery still requires an actual compensable injury. On the accepted characterization of Heck's presented action, dismissal without prejudice stands on lack of present cognizability, without deciding misconduct, immunity, personal responsibility, preclusion, damages, or entitlement to release.

**Proposition-level authority:** Part IV of O'Connor's Opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Blackmun, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas and Ginsburg; all nine directly adopt this boundary, application and remedy.

**Material application:** The lower courts' characterization that Heck's action necessarily attacked the conviction was not disputed in the petition or principal merits brief; the late reply established no separately preserved independent-injury count requiring remand. An unlawful-search claim can remain compatible with a conviction through independent source, inevitable discovery or harmless error, but those possibilities establish neither automatic admissibility of every search claim nor compensation for conviction injury without invalidation.

**Treatment of earlier authority:**

- Wolff v. McDonnell: preserves the distinction between compatible civil relief and relief dependent on invalidating custody.
- Cavanaugh v. Roller, November 30, 1993: distinguishes its opportunity-only claim; the existing §1983 holding remains intact.
- Carey v. Piphus: applies the requirement of actual compensable injury without adjudicating the amount or merits here.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Former prisoners and persons who never had a realistic custody-review opportunity remain outside the question resolved. No universal constitutional malicious-prosecution action, automatic state-law preclusion, automatic habeas conversion or pending-prosecution abstention rule is adopted. Custis's distinct no-meaningful-review reservation in the ACCA channel remains intact. Recognition of a conviction's invalidation by an authorized state tribunal identifies one circumstance in which the present §1983 prerequisite is satisfied. It does not itself create jurisdiction, confer authority to reopen the underlying conviction, or prescribe grounds upon which a state tribunal must grant collateral relief. Those questions remain governed by the independently applicable law authorizing that tribunal's action.

**Operative remedy or transition:** Dismissal without prejudice is affirmed unanimously for lack of present cognizability. The barred claim has not accrued, so its limitations period is not consumed while the prerequisite remains unmet. A later otherwise proper action may follow qualifying invalidation, subject to remaining requirements; no stay, release, automatic conversion to habeas, liability, immunity or damages determination is ordered.

### Independent wage rights and collective bargaining

#### Livadas v. Bradshaw, 512 U.S. 107

**Docket or dockets:** No. 92-1920.

**Decided:** 1994-06-13.
